a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{N^{\prime}}d^{\prime}
and
\Lgtp(d^{\prime}/b,c;N^{\prime})=\Lgtp(d/b,c;N^{\prime})
. Then just pick
(a^{\prime};N)
in an extension
N^{\prime}\to N
such that
\Lgtp(a^{\prime},d/b,c;N)=\Lgtp(a,d^{\prime}/b,c;N)
.
∎

Convention 4.14
.

We call the class function
\Upsilon
for
Club Local Character
a
local character function
. For an object
A
we write
\Upsilon(A)
for
\Upsilon(\lambda)
where
\lambda
is the least regular cardinal such that
A
is
\lambda
-presentable.

Lemma 4.15

(Chain local character)
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation satisfying
Club Local Character
. Let
A\leq N
and
\kappa\geq\Upsilon(A)
. Suppose that we are given a continuous chain
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
of
\kappa
-presentable models with
M=\colim_{i<\kappa}M_{i}\leq N
. Then there is
i_{0}<\kappa
such that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}^{N}M
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\Sub^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{M}}(M)
be the club set from
Club Local Character
. By ?THM?
(ii) the chain
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
forms a club set on
\Sub^{\kappa}_{\mathcal{M}}(M)
. So by ?THM?
(i)
\{M_{i}:i<\kappa\}\cap\mathcal{F}
is nonempty.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

For all our results we only need chain local character. That is, the conclusion of ?THM?
. In particular the canonicity theorems in Section
7
go through even if we would just assume chain local character.

Given that we actually only need chain local character, as per ?THM?
, it is natural to ask whether the converse of ?THM?
holds. That is, if chain local character implies
Club Local Character
. This is not so clear, so we leave it at this.

We recall the following from
[
Kam20
, Definition 6.13]
.
1
1

1

There is a slight improvement here. We additionally require here that
c
embeds in the chain. In
[
Kam20
]
this is the case for every such sequence that is considered. There are only two uses of such sequences. One in Lemma 6.14 where they are constructed, and we indeed get that
c
embeds in the chain of witnesses of independence. The other one is in the main proof, where such a sequence is found from an application of Lemma 6.14.

Definition 4.17
.

Suppose we have an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
. Let
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
be a sequence in some
M
and let
c:C\to M
be an arrow. Suppose that
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
is a chain of initial segments for
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
and that
c
embeds in the chain. Then we call
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}

witnesses of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independence
for
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
if

a_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M}M_{i}

for all
i<\kappa
. We say that a sequence is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independent
if it admits a chain of witnesses of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independence.

The following proposition is the standard argument showing that we can find arbitrarily long independent sequences, assuming very few properties for our independence relation (see e.g.
[
Kim14
, Proposition 2.2.4]
). The proposition after that shows that if we additionally assume
Union
we can actually get arbitrarily long independent isi-sequences.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be an independence relation satisfying
Invariance
,
Existence
and
Extension
. Then for any
(a,c;M)
with
\dom(c)\in\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
and any
\kappa
there is some extension
M\to N
containing a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independent sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
with
\gtp(a_{i},c;N)=\gtp(a,c;M)
for all
i<\kappa
.

Proof.

We construct the witnesses of independence
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
and sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
by induction. At stage
i
we will construct
a_{i}
and
M_{i+1}
. By
Existence
we have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M}c
, and so we will have
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M_{i}}c
for all
i<\kappa
. At every stage we will apply
Extension
to the latter.

For the base case we set
M_{0}=M
and use
Extension
to find
a_{0}
and
M\to M_{1}
with
\gtp(a_{0},c;M_{1})=\gtp(a,c;M)
and
a_{0}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M_{1}}M_{0}
. In the successor step we use
Extension
to find
M_{i+1}\to M_{i+2}
and
a_{i+1}
such that
a_{i+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M_{i+2}}M_{i+1}
and
\gtp(a_{i+1},c;M_{i+2})=\gtp(a,c;M)
. Finally, for limit
\ell<\kappa
let
M_{\ell}=\colim_{i<\ell}M_{i}
. We use
Extension
to find
M_{\ell}\to M_{\ell+1}
and
a_{\ell}
with
\gtp(a_{\ell},c;M_{\ell+1})=\gtp(a,c;M)
and
a_{\ell}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{M_{\ell+1}}M_{\ell}
. We finish the construction by taking
N=\colim_{i<\kappa}M_{i}
.
∎

Proposition 4.19
.

Suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation satisfying
Invariance
,
Existence
,
Extension
and
Union
. Then given
(a,c;M)
with
\dom(c)\in\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
and any
\kappa
, there is a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independent isi-sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
over
c
in some extension
M\to N
such that
\gtp(a_{i},c;N)=\gtp(a,c;M)
for all
i<\kappa
.

Proof.

This is just
[
Kam20
, Lemma 6.14]
. The differences in terminology are discussed after ?THM?
. In particular, the monotonicity assumption there is only necessary to get what we call
Extension
. Finally, there is no notion of base class there, but this is only relevant in the application of
Existence
, which is only applied with
c
in the base. Hence the assumption
\dom(c)\in\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
.
∎

5.
Independence theorem, 3-amalgamation and stationarity

It is well known that the property
Independence Theorem
can also be formulated as an amalgamation property of some independent system. This allows for a more categorical statement without any mention of Lascar strong Galois types. However, we need to restrict ourselves to work only over models. We will give this property its own name and prove its equivalence to
Independence Theorem
, modulo some basic properties, in ?THM?
.

The contents of this section are not necessary for the results in the rest of this paper, but we do refer to them a few times in remarks and discussions.

Definition 5.1

(
[
Kam20
, Definition 6.7]
)
.

An independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
has
3-amalgamation
if the following holds. Suppose that we have

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N_{1}}B,\quad B\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N_{2}}C,\quad C\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N_{3}}A,

overloading notation for subobjects of different models. Suppose furthermore that
M
is a model and that

\displaystyle\gtp(a,m;N_{1})

\displaystyle=\gtp(a,m;N_{3}),

\displaystyle\gtp(b,m;N_{1})

\displaystyle=\gtp(b,m;N_{2}),

\displaystyle\gtp(c,m;N_{2})

\displaystyle=\gtp(c,m;N_{3}),

where
a
,
b
,
c
and
m
are representatives for the subobjects
A
,
B
,
C
and
M
respectively (again, overloading notation for different models). Then we can find extensions from
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
to some
N
such that the diagram we obtain in that way commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

Furthermore, these extensions are such that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N}N_{2}
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be a basic independence relation. If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
Independence Theorem
then it also satisfies
3-amalgamation
. Conversely, if
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies
3-amalgamation
then it satisfies
Independence Theorem
over models (i.e. we require the base
C
to be a model).

Proof.

We first prove that
Independence Theorem
implies
3-amalgamation
. Let the set up be as in ?THM?
. In the diagram below we find the dashed arrows by using
\gtp(c,m;N_{2})=\gtp(c,m;N_{3})
and
\gtp(b,m;N_{1})=\gtp(b,m;N_{2})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

We write
a_{1}
for the arrow
A\to N_{1}\to N^{\prime}
and
a_{3}
for the arrow
A\to N_{3}\to N^{\prime}
. Then we have
\Lgtp(a_{1}/m;N^{\prime})=\Lgtp(a_{3}/m;N^{\prime})
. We can thus apply
Independence Theorem
to find some extension
N^{\prime}\to N^{*}
with some
a^{*}:A\to N^{*}
such that
\Lgtp(a^{*},b/m;N^{*})=\Lgtp(a_{1},b/m;N^{*})
,
\Lgtp(a^{*},c/m;N^{*})=\Lgtp(a_{3},c/m;N^{*})
and
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N^{*}}N^{\prime}
. So in particular we have
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M}^{N^{*}}N_{2}
by
Monotonicity
. Using
\gtp(a,b,m;N_{1})=\gtp(a_{1},b,m;N^{*})=\gtp(a^{*},b,m;N^{*})
and
\gtp(a,c,m;N_{3})=\gtp(a_{3},c,m;N^{*})=\gtp(a^{*},c,m;N^{*})
after each other we find an extension
N^{*}\to N
together with extensions from
N_{1}
and
N_{3}
to
N
and we just forget about the previous extensions from
N_{1}
and
N_{3}
to
N^{*}
. These two new extensions, together with
N_{2}\to N^{*}\to N
, then form the solution to our
3-amalgamation
problem.

Now we prove the converse. So we assume
3-amalgamation
and we prove
Independence Theorem
over models. So suppose that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{m}^{N}b
,
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{m}^{N}c
and
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{m}^{N}c
and
\Lgtp(a/m;N)=\Lgtp(a^{\prime}/m;N)
. Then we can form the commuting diagram as below, where we find the dashed arrows by
3-amalgamation
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

We take the extension
N\to N^{*}
to be
h
and write
a^{*}
for
fa=ga^{\prime}
. The application of
3-amalgamation
yields
a^{*}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{m}^{N^{*}}N
. Furthermore,
\gtp(a^{*},b,m;N^{*})=\gtp(a,b,m;N)=\gtp(a,b,m;N^{*})
, so
\Lgtp(a^{*},b/m;N^{*})=\Lgtp(a,b/m;N^{*})
because the domain of
m
is a model. Similarly, we also find
\Lgtp(a^{*},c/m;N^{*})=\Lgtp(a^{\prime},c/m;N^{*})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We recall that
3-amalgamation
follows from
Stationarity
. This might for example be useful in the case where
\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})=\mathcal{M}
, so one does not need to verify the
Independence Theorem
property for a stable independence relation, as it follows automatically. In particular this means that a stable independence relation in the sense of
[
LRV19
]
also yields a stable independence relation in our setting and vice versa, see also
[
Kam20
, Remark 6.7]
for further comparison.

Fact 5.3

(
[
Kam20
, Proposition 6.16]
)
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be a basic independence relation satisfying
Stationarity
then it also satisfies
3-amalgamation
.

6.
Long dividing, isi-dividing and long Kim-dividing

In this section we introduce various notions of dividing, each yielding its own independence relation. These notions are based on the classical notion of dividing, as we know it from first-order logic. For the convenience of the reader, and to compare it to the new definitions, we recall the definition of dividing.

Definition 6.1
.

In the classical setting of first-order logic, we say that a type
p(x,b)=\tp(a/Cb)

divides over
C
if there is a
C
-indiscernible sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
such that
\tp(b_{i}/C)=\tp(b/C)
for all
i<\omega
and
\bigcup_{i<\omega}p(x,b_{i})
is inconsistent.

In many proofs, to use this definition, one has to apply compactness in one way or another. For example to elongate the sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
, or to find a finite subsequence along which
p
is inconsistent. This is generally an issue in AECats, because we do not have compactness there. To solve this we introduce the notion of long dividing. The name is due to the fact that we consider arbitrarily long sequences in the definition, something that we would normally have to use compactness for. This is very close to the notion of isi-dividing from
[
Kam20
, Definition 5.7]
. In fact, isi-dividing is just long dividing but then restricted to isi-sequences, so that we have some homogeneity in the sequences involved. Of course, indiscernible sequences would be even more homogeneous, but the little bit that isi-sequences offer us turns out to be enough.

We remind the reader of ?THM?
, namely that all cardinals considered are regular. So in the following definition we only quantify over regular cardinals.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
be an AECat with AP and fix some
(a,b,c;M)
.

(i)

Suppose that we have some sequence
((b_{i})_{i\in I};M)
such that
\gtp(b_{i},c;M)=\gtp(b,c;M)
for all
i\in I
. We say that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is
consistent
for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
if there is an extension
M\to N
and an arrow
(a^{\prime};N)
such that

\gtp(a,b,c;M)=\gtp(a^{\prime},b_{i},c;N)

for all
i\in I
. We call
a^{\prime}
a
realisation
of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
.

Being inconsistent is the negation of the above. So
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is
inconsistent
for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
if there is no extension of
M
with a realisation
a^{\prime}
of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
.

(ii)

We say that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)

long divides
over
c
if there is
\mu
such that for every
\lambda\geq\mu
there is a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
in some extension
M\to N
with
\gtp(b_{i},c;N)=\gtp(b,c;M)
for all
i<\lambda
, such that for some
\kappa<\lambda
and every
I\subseteq\lambda
with
|I|=\kappa
we have that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
.

(iii)

We say that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)

isi-divides
if it long divides with respect to isi-sequences over
c
. That is, we require the sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
to be an isi-sequence over
c
.

Remark 6.3
.

We discussed how long dividing and isi-dividing are inspired by dividing. A natural question would be whether or not they are actually the same. This was discussed in
[
Kam20
]
from Remark 5.8 and onwards. The discussion there is just about isi-dividing but applies to long dividing as well. The summary is as follows, restricting ourselves to AECats obtained from a first-order theory.

(i)

Dividing implies isi-dividing implies long dividing.

(ii)

Long dividing (and thus isi-dividing) implies dividing if we assume the existence of a proper class of Ramsey cardinals.

(iii)

Using the canonicity theorem, ?THM?
, we can actually drop the large cardinal assumption and conclude that isi-dividing implies dividing in simple theories.

(iv)

The question remains: does long dividing (and thus isi-dividing) generally imply dividing, without the large cardinal assumption?

In fact, dividing makes sense in positive logic as well (see
[
Pil00
,
BY03b
]
), and the above discussion applies to AECats obtained from a positive theory as well. There is an even more general setting, namely that of finitely short AECats, where this discussion applies. These are AECats where a Galois type of an infinite tuple of arrows is determined by the Galois types of the finite subtuples, see
[
Kam21
, Section 4]
. This is more or less the same framework as that of homogeneous model theory in the sense of
[
BL03
]
(see
[
Kam20
, Example 4.4]
).

The point of these dividing notions is that they yield independence relations. We can already prove some basic properties about these independence relations in arbitrary AECats, similar to the basic properties that dividing always has.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
A,B,C\leq M
be subobjects. Let
(a,b,c;M)
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};M)
be two sets of representatives. Then
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
long divides over
c
if and only if
\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};M)
long divides over
c^{\prime}
. The same statement holds for isi-dividing.

Proof.

This comes down to checking all the definitions, which is lengthy to do in detail. However, there is only one trick that we repeatedly use, and that is ?THM?
(ii). To apply this trick we let
f,g,h
be isomorphisms such that
a^{\prime}=af
,
b^{\prime}=bg
and
c^{\prime}=ch
. Then, using the above trick, we easily see that for any sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
witnessing long dividing of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
we have that
(b_{i}g)_{i<\lambda}
witnesses long dividing for
\gtp(a,bg,c;M)=\gtp(a,b^{\prime},c;M)
. Similarly we can replace
c
by
ch=c^{\prime}
and
a
by
af=a^{\prime}
. The same holds for isi-dividing, noting that any isi-sequence over
c
is also an isi-sequence over
ch=c^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 6.5
.

For subobjects
A,B,C\leq M
we write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{ld},M}B
if
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not long divide for all (equivalently: some) representatives
a,b,c
of
A,B,C
. Similarly, we write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-d},M}B
if
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not isi-divide.

As
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\textup{ld}}
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\textup{isi-d}}
do not generally satisfy
Symmetry
we have to distinguish between “left” and “right” versions of certain properties, as we do below.

Proposition 6.6
.

Long dividing and isi-dividing always satisfy the following:
Invariance
,
Left-Monotonicity
,
Existence
and
Base-Monotonicity
. In addition, long dividing also satisfies
Right-Monotonicity

Proof.

Everything is direct from the definition, except for
Right-Monotonicity
for long dividing and
Base-Monotonicity
. For both we will prove the contraposition.

For
Base-Monotonicity
let
(a,b,c,c^{\prime};M)
be such that
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
long divides over
c^{\prime}
and
C\leq C^{\prime}\leq B
, where
C,C^{\prime},B
are the subobjects represented by
c,c^{\prime},b
respectively. Let
\mu
be as in the definition of long dividing and let
\lambda\geq\mu
. Then there is
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
in some
N
that witnesses long dividing of
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
over
c^{\prime}
. We will prove that it also witnesses long dividing of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
. Indeed we have for all
i<\lambda
that
\gtp(b_{i},c^{\prime};N)=\gtp(b,c^{\prime};M)
and thus
\gtp(b_{i},c;N)=\gtp(b,c;M)
, because
C\leq C^{\prime}
. Let
\kappa<\lambda
be such that for
I\subseteq\lambda
with
|I|=\kappa
we have that
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. We claim that for such
I
we also have that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. Suppose that there would be a realisation
(a^{\prime};N^{\prime})
for some extension
N\to N^{\prime}
, then
\gtp(a^{\prime},b_{i},c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c;M)
for all
i\in I
. Since
c^{\prime}
factors through
b
and
b_{i}
in the same way for all
i<\lambda
, see ?THM?
, we then have
\gtp(a^{\prime},b_{i},c^{\prime};N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
for all
i\in I
, contradicting that
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. This proves
Base-Monotonicity
for long dividing. We have shown that the same sequences that witness long dividing of
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
also witness long dividing of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
. As any isi-sequence over
c^{\prime}
is an isi-sequence over
c
, the same proof shows that isi-dividing has
Base-Monotonicity
.

Now we prove
Right-Monotonicity
for long dividing. Let
(a,b,b^{\prime},c;M)
be such that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
long divides over
c
and
b
factors through
b^{\prime}
. For any sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
in some
N
witnessing long dividing we can form
(b_{i}^{\prime})_{i<\lambda}
by letting
b_{i}^{\prime}
be such that
\gtp(b_{i}^{\prime},b_{i},c;N)=\gtp(b^{\prime},b,c;M)
for all
i<\lambda
(possibly replacing
N
by an extension in the process). Then for
I\subseteq\lambda
a realisation of
\gtp(a,b^{\prime},c;M)
for
(b_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
would also be a realisation of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. So if we let
\kappa<\lambda
be such that for every
I\subseteq\lambda
with
|I|=\kappa
we have that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
, we also get that
\gtp(a,b^{\prime},c;M)
is inconsistent for
(b_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
for any such
I
. We conclude that
\gtp(a,b^{\prime},c;M)
long divides over
c
.
∎

We note that in the above proof we did not have to change the sequence involved for
Base-Monotonicity
, which was why the same proof also works for isi-dividing. In the proof of
Right-Monotonicity
we had to build a new sequence, which might not be an isi-sequence again. This is why that proof only works for long dividing.

An important property that misses in ?THM?
is
Extension
. Classically (working in a monster model) this is fixed by considering forking instead of dividing. This forces the
Extension
property as follows. Suppose that
Extension
fails for some type
p=\tp(a/Cb)
. Then there is some parameter set, say
D
, such that every extension of
p
to
D
divides over
C
. In other words,
p
implies a disjunction of types over
D
such that every type in that disjunction divides over
C
. Classically we could even further reformulate this by using compactness and having
p
actually imply a finite disjunction of dividing formulas, but that is not necessary and we want to avoid compactness in our definitions. So our definition of isi-forking will be the semantical way of saying “implies a (possibly infinite) disjunction of types that each isi-divide”.

Definition 6.7
.

We say that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)

isi-forks
over
c
if there is some extension
M\to N
with
((a_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
such that:

(i)

\gtp(a_{j},d_{j},c;N)
isi-divides over
c
for each
j\in J
;

(ii)

given an extension
N\to N^{\prime}
with some
(a^{\prime};N^{\prime})
such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c;N)
there is
j\in J
such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},d_{j},c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a_{j},d_{j},c;N)
.

Note that we do not require that
b
actually factors through the
d_{j}
. This is because we also want to force in
Right-Monotonicity
.

Of course, one could also define a notion of
long forking
by replacing isi-dividing by long dividing in the above. However, we will have no use for this.

Remark 6.8
.

The definition of isi-forking is just the semantical way of saying “
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
implies a (possibly infinite) disjunction of Galois types that each isi-divide over
c
”. In the first-order setting and in the positive setting (see
[
Pil00
]
) forking has been defined and can be formulated as follows: a type forks over
C
if it implies a (possibly infinite) disjunction of types that each divide over
C
. It should then be clear that forking implies isi-forking. This uses the fact that dividing implies isi-dividing, see ?THM?
(i). If isi-dividing and dividing coincide then the converse is true, so isi-forking would then imply forking.

As before, we will prove various basic properties of isi-forking.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
A,B,C\leq M
be subobjects. Let
(a,b,c;M)
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};M)
be two sets of representatives. Then
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
isi-forks over
c
if and only if
\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};M)
isi-forks over
c^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We use the same trick as we did in ?THM?
. Let
f
be the isomorphism such that
a^{\prime}=af
. Let
M\to N
be an extension with
((a_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
witnessing isi-forking of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
. Then using ?THM?
we have that
\gtp(a_{j}f,d_{j},c^{\prime};N)
isi-divides over
c^{\prime}
for all
j\in J
. We claim that isi-forking of
\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};M)
is witnessed by
((a_{j}f)_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
, for which we are now left to check (ii) from ?THM?
.

Let
g
and
h
be isomorphisms such that
b^{\prime}=bg
and
c^{\prime}=ch
. Let
N\to N^{*}
be an extension with some
(a^{*};N^{*})
such that
\gtp(a^{*},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};N^{*})=\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime};N)
. Then

\displaystyle\gtp(a^{*}f^{-1},b,c;N^{*})

\displaystyle=\gtp(a^{*}f^{-1},b^{\prime}g^{-1},c^{\prime}h^{-1};N^{*})

\displaystyle=\gtp(a^{\prime}f^{-1},b^{\prime}g^{-1},c^{\prime}h^{-1};N)

\displaystyle=\gtp(a,b,c;N),

so there is
j\in J
with
\gtp(a^{*}f^{-1},d_{j},c;N^{*})=\gtp(a_{j},d_{j},c;N)
. We conclude that
\gtp(a^{*},d_{j},c^{\prime};N^{*})=\gtp(a^{*}f^{-1}f,d_{j},ch;N^{*})=\gtp(a_{j}f,d_{j},c^{\prime};N)
.
∎

Definition 6.10
.

For
A,B,C\leq M
we write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-f},M}B
if
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not isi-fork for all (equivalently: some) representatives
a,b,c
of
A,B,C
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Isi-forking satisfies the following:
Invariance
,
Monotonicity
on both sides,
Extension
and
Base-Monotonicity
.

Proof.

The properties
Invariance
and
Right-Monotonicity
are direct from the definition. We prove the contraposition of the remaining three.

For
Left-Monotonicity
suppose that
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;M)
isi-forks over
c
and let
(a;M)
be such that
a^{\prime}
factors through
a
. Let
((a^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
in some extension
M\to N
witness the isi-forking. Let
f
be such that
af=a^{\prime}
. The following is a set by ?THM?
:

\displaystyle F=\{\gtp(a^{*},d_{j},c;N^{*}):\,

\displaystyle N^{*}\text{ is an extension of }N\text{ and }j\in J\text{ and}

\displaystyle\gtp(a^{*},b,c;N^{*})=\gtp(a,b,c;M)\text{ and }

\displaystyle\gtp(a^{*}f,d_{j},c;N^{*})=\gtp(a_{j}^{\prime},d_{j},c;N)\}.

By
Left-Monotonicity
of isi-dividing, every Galois type in
F
isi-divides over
c
. By inductively amalgamating things we find one extension
N\to N^{*}
with
((a_{k})_{k\in K},(d_{k})_{k\in K};N^{*})
such that every Galois type in
F
is realised by
(a_{k},d_{k},c;N^{*})
for some
k\in K
. This then witnesses isi-forking of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
over
c
.

For
Extension
let
(a,b,b^{\prime},c;M)
be such that
b
factors through
b^{\prime}
and for every
(a^{\prime};N)
in some extension
M\to N
with
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;N)=\gtp(a,b,c;M)
we have that
\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c;N)
isi-forks over
c
. So the conclusion of the
Extension
property for
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
fails. We have to prove that then
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
isi-forks over
c
.

For each Galois type in
\Sgtp(\dom(a),\dom(b^{\prime}),\dom(c))
we fix some witnesses of isi-forking. By ?THM?
and the definition of isi-forking, the following is a set:

\displaystyle F=\{

\displaystyle\gtp(a^{\prime},d,c;N):N\text{ is an extension of }M\text{ and}

\displaystyle\quad\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;N)=\gtp(a,b,c;M)\text{ and }

\displaystyle\quad\gtp(a^{\prime},d,c;N)\text{ is a fixed witness of isi-forking of }\gtp(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c;N)\}.

By inductively amalgamating things we find one extension
N\to N^{*}
together with
((a_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N^{*})
such that every Galois type in
F
is realised by
(a_{j},d_{j},c;N^{*})
for some
j\in J
. This then witnesses isi-forking of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
over
c
.

Finally, for
Base-Monotonicity
let
(a,b,c,c^{\prime};M)
be such that
\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};M)
isi-forks over
c^{\prime}
and
C\leq C^{\prime}\leq B
, where
C,C^{\prime},B
are the subobjects represented by
c,c^{\prime},b
respectively. Let
((a_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
witness this in some extension
M\to N
. We claim that this also witnesses isi-forking of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
over
c
. Indeed, let
a^{\prime}:A\to N^{\prime}
for some extension
N\to N^{\prime}
be such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c;N)
. We have
C^{\prime}\leq B
, so
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c^{\prime};N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c^{\prime};N)
. So there must be some
j\in J
such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},d_{j},c^{\prime};N^{\prime})=\gtp(a_{j},d_{j},c^{\prime};N)
. As
C\leq C^{\prime}
this restricts to
\gtp(a^{\prime},d_{j},c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a_{j},d_{j},c;N)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

For any
A,B,C\leq M
we always have

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-f},M}B\implies A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-d},M}B.

The converse holds if and only if isi-dividing satisfies
Right-Monotonicity
and
Extension
.

Proof.

The first implication is just the contrapositive of the trivial statement that isi-dividing implies isi-forking. If the converse of this implication holds, then isi-dividing and isi-forking coincide and so isi-dividing satisfies
Right-Monotonicity
and
Extension
by ?THM?
.

We are left to prove that if isi-dividing satisfies
Right-Monotonicity
and
Extension
then isi-forking implies isi-dividing. Suppose for a contradiction that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
isi-forks over
c
but does not isi-divide over
c
. Let
((a_{j})_{j\in J},(d_{j})_{j\in J};N)
for some extension
M\to N
witness the isi-forking of
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
. By
Extension
for isi-dividing we find an extension
N\to N^{\prime}
with
(a^{\prime};N^{\prime})
such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},b,c;N^{\prime})=\gtp(a,b,c;N)
and
\gtp(a^{\prime},N,c;N^{\prime})
does not isi-divide. By isi-forking, there must be
j\in J
such that
\gtp(a^{\prime},d_{j},c;N^{\prime})
isi-divides over
c
contradicting
Right-Monotonicity
of isi-dividing.
∎

When considering NSOP
1
-theories in first-order logic the useful notion of independence is given by Kim-dividing, see for example
[
KR20
,
DKR22
]
. The idea is to only consider dividing with respect to Morley sequences, that is, with respect to indiscernible nonforking sequences. We adapt that definition to our earlier ideas.

Definition 6.13
.

We say that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)

long Kim-divides
over
c
if it long divides over
c
with respect to
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{\textup{isi-f}}
-independent sequences. That is, the definition is exactly as long dividing, but we require the sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
to be
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{\textup{isi-f}}
-independent. We write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{lK},M}B
if
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not long Kim-divide over
c
for all (equivalently: some) representatives
a,b,c
of the subobjects
A,B,C
.

We implicitly used a combination of ?THM?
and ?THM?
to conclude that long Kim-dividing is invariant under taking different representatives of subobjects.

We have defined
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{lK}}
-independence using
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-f}}
-independent sequences, but these may not exist. For this we define the following axiom, from which the existence of such sequences follows.

Definition 6.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
be an AECat and let
\mathcal{B}
be a base class. We say that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
satisfies the
\mathcal{B}
-existence axiom
if
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\textup{isi-f}}
with its base restricted to
\mathcal{B}
satisfies
Existence
. That is, for all
A,C\leq M
with
C\in\mathcal{B}
we have
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-f},M}C
.

Corollary 6.15
.

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
satisfies the
\mathcal{B}
-existence axiom then for any
(a,c;M)
with
\dom(c)\in\mathcal{B}
and any
\kappa
there is some extension
M\to N
containing a
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{\textup{isi-f}}
-independent sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\kappa}
with
\gtp(a_{i},c;N)=\gtp(a,c;M)
for all
i<\kappa
.

Proof.

Combine ?THM?
and ?THM?
.
∎

Example 6.16
.

We discuss some examples of the
\mathcal{B}
-existence axiom. These are either settings where we have the axiom, or where it is natural to assume the axiom.

(i)

For any first-order theory
T
we have the
\mathbf{Mod}(T)
-existence axiom. This is because any type over a model can be extended to a global invariant type, which can then be used in a standard argument to show that such a type does not isi-fork over
M
as follows.

Let
q(x)\supseteq\tp(a/M)
be a global
M
-invariant extension. Let
\alpha\models q
, which then lives in some bigger monster model. Suppose that
\tp(a/M)
isi-forks. Then there is
d
such that
r(x,d)=\tp(\alpha/Md)
isi-divides over
M
. However, we will show that
r(x,d)
cannot even long divide. So let
(d_{i})_{i<\lambda}
be any infinite sequence in
\tp(d/M)
. Then by
M
-invariance
\tp(\alpha d_{i}/M)=\tp(\alpha d/M)
for all
i<\lambda
. So
\bigcup_{i<\lambda}r(x,d_{i})
is consistent, and so we conclude that
r(x,d)
does not long divide.

(ii)

Analogous to the previous point, for a continuous theory
T
we have the
\mathbf{MetMod}(T)
-existence axiom.

(iii)

For positive logic something similar to (i) is true, but we need an extra assumption on
T
. We recall these assumptions in more detail in ?THM?
. For now we just summarise what we get from them in terms of the existence axiom. In a semi-Hausdorff positive theory
T
any type over an e.c. model can be extended to a global invariant type (see
[
BY03c
, Lemma 3.11]
), so the proof in (i) goes through and we have the
\mathbf{Mod}(T)
-existence axiom. In the more general class of thick theories we still have the
\mathbf{Mod}(T)
-existence axiom, but we have to use global Lascar-invariant types instead, see
[
DK22
, Lemma 3.11 and Lemma 9.11]
.

(iv)

For an NSOP
1
theory
T
in first-order logic it is common to assume the existence axiom for forking. It is still an open problem whether or not the existence axiom for forking holds in every NSOP
1
theory
T
, but it has been proved in many specific instances, see
[
DKR22
, Fact 2.14]
.

If for such
T
we take
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})=(\mathbf{SubMod}(T),\mathbf{Mod}(T))
then we are very close to having the
\mathcal{C}
-existence axiom. The only difference is that we work with isi-forking, see ?THM?
for a comparison. In particular, the
\mathcal{C}
-existence axiom implies the existence axiom for forking. Furthermore, if there is a proper class of Ramsey cardinals then isi-forking and forking coincide and so the converse would hold as well. Additionally, it is quite likely that techniques to prove existence for forking also work for isi-forking. For example, in
[
DKR22
, Remark 2.15]
it is shown that in the theory of parametrised equivalence relations any type over any set
A
can be extended to a global
A
-invariant type. Following point (i) we then see that such a type does not isi-fork over
A
.

(v)

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
is an AECat with a simple independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
then it will satisfy the
\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
-existence axiom. This follows from canonicity, ?THM?
, because then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\textup{isi-f}}
over
\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
. This mirrors the fact that simple theories in first-order logic (and even simple thick positive theories, see
[
BY03c
]
) have the existence axiom for forking, see also the previous point.

Remark 6.17
.

The usual definition of Kim-dividing states that a type Kim-divides if it divides with respect to non-forking Morley sequences, see e.g.
[
KR20
,
DKR22
]
. To compare this to long Kim-dividing we first note that by ?THM?
any
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\textup{isi-f}}
-independent sequence is also a forking-independent sequence, and the converse is true if isi-dividing coincides with dividing. As before, if we assume that there is a proper class of Ramsey cardinals then long Kim-dividing and Kim-dividing coincide, using the same arguments as for long dividing and isi-dividing versus dividing.

If we do not want to assume large cardinals then we can again use canonicity, this time ?THM?
, to see that long Kim-dividing and Kim-dividing coincide in NSOP
1
-theories where it has been developed.

7.
Canonicity

In this section we prove the main results, namely the canonicity theorems for simple (?THM?
) and NSOP
1
-like (?THM?
) independence relations. The former is just a slightly improved version of
[
Kam20
, Theorem 1.1]
. The results in this section up to and including ?THM?
are then essentially just the proof of
[
Kam20
, Theorem 1.1]
cut up in smaller parts. However, we cannot really just refer to that proof again. First of all because we work with slightly different definitions. More importantly, the results here are actually improved versions. Most notably ?THM?
gives a lot more information, where the proof of
[
Kam20
, Theorem 1.1]
only used the last sentence of that lemma.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
be an AECat with AP and let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be a basic independence relation that also satisfies
Club Local Character
. Then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{isi-d},M}B
implies
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{M}B
for any
C\in\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
.

If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies the same assumptions, except possibly
Union
, then we still have that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{\textup{ld},M}B
implies
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{C}^{M}B
for any
C\in\base(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not isi-divide over
c
. Let
\kappa\geq\Upsilon(A)
such that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M})
is a
\kappa
-AECat and
\dom(a)
and
\dom(c)
are
\kappa
-presentable. By ?THM?
we find a long enough
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independent isi-sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\lambda}
over
c
in some
M\to N
with
\lambda>\kappa
and
\gtp(b_{i},c;N)=\gtp(b,c;M)
for all
i<\lambda
. Let
(M_{i})_{i<\lambda}
be witnesses of independence. Since
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
does not isi-divide over
c
there is
I\subseteq\lambda
with
|I|=\kappa
such that
\gtp(a,b,c;M)
is consistent for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
. Let
a^{\prime}
be a realisation for
(b_{i})_{i\in I}
, which for convenience we may assume to be in
N
. By possibly deleting an end segment from
I
we may assume that
I
has the order type of
\kappa
. Using ?THM?
we may assume that each object in the chain
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
is
\kappa
-presentable, where
Monotonicity
implies that these are still witnesses of independence. Then by chain local character, ?THM?
, we find
i_{0}\in I
such that
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}^{N}M_{I}
where
M_{I}=\colim_{i\in I}M_{i}
. By
Monotonicity
and
Symmetry
we then have

b_{i_{0}}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{M_{i_{0}}}^{N}a^{\prime}.

We also have

b_{i_{0}}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{N}M_{i_{0}}.

So by
Transitivity
we have
b_{i_{0}}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}^{N}a^{\prime}
and the result follows by
Symmetry
and because
\gtp(a^{\prime},b_{i_{0}},c;N)=\gtp(a,b,c;M)
.

For the final claim we just note that if we do not have
Union
we can still apply ?THM?
instead of ?THM?
to get an arbitrarily long
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}_{c}
-independent sequence. It might just not be an isi-sequence. Then the rest of the proof goes through as written.
∎

The following lemma generalises the
Independence Theorem
property to independent sequences of any length. The original
Independence Theorem
can roughly be viewed as just considering an independent sequence of length two.

Lemma 7.2

(Generalised independence theorem)
.

Suppose that
